From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I came in because I seem to be spending far too much of my day checking things. I keep opening the same folders, the drawers, my labeled boxes, just... again and again. \n\nI just have to make sure that my important papers and keepsakes are exactly where I left them. And the frustrating part is, I'll do this even when I just checked them a few minutes before. It's taking up too much of my time, and I needed to speak to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to follow a specific routine or pattern when you're checking these things?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, the pattern is really just... doing the exact same thing over and over. I go to those same folders, the drawers, the boxes I have labeled. I just have to physically see those important papers and my keepsakes to be absolutely sure they're still sitting right where I put them. So, I suppose the routine is just that—going right back to open them and check again, even if I literally just looked at them a few minutes ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I logically know better, a thought will just keep needling me out of nowhere. I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or... or that I put a special family recipe in the wrong box. I just can't seem to let the thought pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless, and that's usually what makes me go back and check.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check these things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm not worried that... that a disaster will strike or someone will get hurt if I don't check. It's not like that. It's really just what I mentioned before. I just get this overwhelming feeling that I've lost something important or made a careless mistake. I don't have any fears about something terrible happening, beyond just the frustration of losing the papers themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to keep your belongings in a very specific order or arrangement?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I have my labeled boxes and folders so I know where things are, but I don't feel like they have to be perfectly lined up or arranged in a certain way. It's just about knowing the items are still inside them, not how they look on the shelf.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like checking these things will prevent something bad from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Well, no, like I was just saying... it's really not about that at all. I don't think checking the boxes is going to save anyone's life or keep someone from getting hurt. I don't have any fears like that. It's just about making sure I haven't lost my husband's papers or my recipes. That's really all it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be in a specific order or position within their containers?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, as long as they're inside the folder or the box, I really don't care how they're stacked. I don't need them alphabetized or facing a certain way. It's just... well, like I said, it's just about seeing that the papers are actually in there and haven't gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way or a certain number of times to feel complete?\n\nPatient: No, I don't count or anything like that. There's no special routine to it. I just open the box or the folder, look inside, and once I see that the papers are still there, I'm done. I don't have to do it a certain number of times to feel right... I just have to see them with my own eyes so I know they aren't lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking helps you feel more balanced or even in some way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I'm not sure what you mean by feeling balanced or even. It doesn't do anything like that for me. It just... well, it just settles my mind about whether I've lost those papers. Once I see them, I know they aren't gone, and that's really the only feeling I get from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be symmetrical or evenly arranged in any way?\n\nPatient: No, they don't need to be symmetrical. Like I mentioned, I really don't care how they look in the folder or the box. They can be crooked or in a messy pile for all I care. I mean, as long as they're inside the folder or the box, I really don't care how they're stacked. I don't need them alphabetized or facing a certain way. It's just... well, like I said, it's just about seeing that the papers are actually in there and haven't gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way or a certain number of times to feel complete?\n\nPatient: No, I don't count or anything like that. There's no special routine to it. I just open the box or the folder, look inside, and once I see that the papers are still there, I'm done. I don't have to do it a certain number of times to feel right... I just have to see them with my own eyes so I know they aren't lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking helps you feel more balanced or even in some way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I'm not sure what you mean by feeling balanced or even. It doesn't do anything like that for me. It just... well, it just settles my mind about whether I've lost those papers. Once I see them, I know they aren't gone, and that's really the only feeling I get from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be symmetrical or evenly arranged in any way?\n\nPatient: No, they don't need to be symmetrical. Like I mentioned, I really don't care how they look in the folder or the box. They can be crooked or in a messy pile for all I care. I just need to see that they are still there and I haven't misplaced them. The way they're arranged doesn't bother me one bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or unease in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it does help ease my mind for a moment. Once I see them, I know they aren't gone, and that's really the only feeling I get from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be symmetrical or evenly arranged in any way?\n\nPatient: No, they don't need to be symmetrical. Like I mentioned, I really don't care how they look in the folder or the box. They can be crooked or in a messy pile for all I care. I just need to see that they are still there and I haven't misplaced them. The way they're arranged doesn't bother me one bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or unease in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it does help ease my mind for a moment. But I suppose that's exactly why I came in to see you. I spend far too much of my day opening those same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again. I just have to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are where I left them. The problem is, the unease comes right back... and I end up checking them all over again, even when I just checked a few minutes before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things because you're afraid of making a mistake, like misfiling something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that is exactly it. Even when I... well, even when I know better, this thought will just keep needling me. I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or maybe I put a family recipe in the wrong box somewhere. It just gets stuck in my head, and I can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. So, I have to go look just to prove to myself that I didn't make a mistake.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I came in because I seem to be spending far too much of my day checking things. I keep opening the same folders, the drawers, my labeled boxes, just... again and again. \n\nI just have to make sure that my important papers and keepsakes are exactly where I left them. And the frustrating part is, I'll do this even when I just checked them a few minutes before. It's taking up too much of my time, and I needed to speak to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to follow a specific routine or pattern when you're checking these things?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, the pattern is really just... doing the exact same thing over and over. I go to those same folders, the drawers, the boxes I have labeled. I just have to physically see those important papers and my keepsakes to be absolutely sure they're still sitting right where I put them. So, I suppose the routine is just that—going right back to open them and check again, even if I literally just looked at them a few minutes ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I logically know better, a thought will just keep needling me out of nowhere. I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or... or that I put a special family recipe in the wrong box. I just can't seem to let the thought pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless, and that's usually what makes me go back and check.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check these things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm not worried that... that a disaster will strike or someone will get hurt if I don't check. It's not like that. It's really just what I mentioned before. I just get this overwhelming feeling that I've lost something important or made a careless mistake. I don't have any fears about something terrible happening, beyond just the frustration of losing the papers themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to keep your belongings in a very specific order or arrangement?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I have my labeled boxes and folders so I know where things are, but I don't feel like they have to be perfectly lined up or arranged in a certain way. It's just about knowing the items are still inside them, not how they look on the shelf.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like checking these things will prevent something bad from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Well, no, like I was just saying... it's really not about that at all. I don't think checking the boxes is going to save anyone's life or keep someone from getting hurt. I don't have any fears like that. It's just about making sure I haven't lost my husband's papers or my recipes. That's really all it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be in a specific order or position within their containers?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, as long as they're inside the folder or the box, I really don't care how they're stacked. I don't need them alphabetized or facing a certain way. It's just... well, like I said, it's just about seeing that the papers are actually in there and haven't gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way or a certain number of times to feel complete?\n\nPatient: No, I don't count or anything like that. There's no special routine to it. I just open the box or the folder, look inside, and once I see that the papers are still there, I'm done. I don't have to do it a certain number of times to feel right... I just have to see them with my own eyes so I know they aren't lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking helps you feel more balanced or even in some way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I'm not sure what you mean by feeling balanced or even. It doesn't do anything like that for me. It just... well, it just settles my mind about whether I've lost those papers. Once I see them, I know they aren't gone, and that's really the only feeling I get from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be symmetrical or evenly arranged in any way?\n\nPatient: No, they don't need to be symmetrical. Like I mentioned, I really don't care how they look in the folder or the box. They can be crooked or in a messy pile for all I care. I just need to see that they are still there and I haven't misplaced them. The way they're arranged doesn't bother me one bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or unease in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it does help ease my mind for a moment. But I suppose that's exactly why I came in to see you. I spend far too much of my day opening those same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again. I just have to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are where I left them. The problem is, the unease comes right back... and I end up checking them all over again, even when I just checked a few minutes before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things because you're afraid of making a mistake, like misfiling something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that is exactly it. Even when I... well, even when I know better, this thought will just keep needling me. I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or maybe I put a family recipe in the wrong box somewhere. It just gets stuck in my head, and I can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. So, I have to go look just to prove to myself that I didn't make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things because you're trying to control something or prevent a mistake from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just mentioned, it's really just about the mistakes. I don't think I'm trying to... control things in general, exactly. I just get so worried that I've lost something or thrown a paper away by accident. So I wouldn't say it's about needing control, just making sure I didn't mess up what I already have.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I came in because I seem to be spending far too much of my day checking things. I keep opening the same folders, the drawers, my labeled boxes, just... again and again. \n\nI just have to make sure that my important papers and keepsakes are exactly where I left them. And the frustrating part is, I'll do this even when I just checked them a few minutes before. It's taking up too much of my time, and I needed to speak to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to follow a specific routine or pattern when you're checking these things?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, the pattern is really just... doing the exact same thing over and over. I go to those same folders, the drawers, the boxes I have labeled. I just have to physically see those important papers and my keepsakes to be absolutely sure they're still sitting right where I put them. So, I suppose the routine is just that—going right back to open them and check again, even if I literally just looked at them a few minutes ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I logically know better, a thought will just keep needling me out of nowhere. I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or... or that I put a special family recipe in the wrong box. I just can't seem to let the thought pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless, and that's usually what makes me go back and check.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check these things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm not worried that... that a disaster will strike or someone will get hurt if I don't check. It's not like that. It's really just what I mentioned before. I just get this overwhelming feeling that I've lost something important or made a careless mistake. I don't have any fears about something terrible happening, beyond just the frustration of losing the papers themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to keep your belongings in a very specific order or arrangement?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I have my labeled boxes and folders so I know where things are, but I don't feel like they have to be perfectly lined up or arranged in a certain way. It's just about knowing the items are still inside them, not how they look on the shelf.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like checking these things will prevent something bad from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Well, no, like I was just saying... it's really not about that at all. I don't think checking the boxes is going to save anyone's life or keep someone from getting hurt. I don't have any fears like that. It's just about making sure I haven't lost my husband's papers or my recipes. That's really all it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be in a specific order or position within their containers?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, as long as they're inside the folder or the box, I really don't care how they're stacked. I don't need them alphabetized or facing a certain way. It's just... well, like I said, it's just about seeing that the papers are actually in there and haven't gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way or a certain number of times to feel complete?\n\nPatient: No, I don't count or anything like that. There's no special routine to it. I just open the box or the folder, look inside, and once I see that the papers are still there, I'm done. I don't have to do it a certain number of times to feel right... I just have to see them with my own eyes so I know they aren't lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking helps you feel more balanced or even in some way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I'm not sure what you mean by feeling balanced or even. It doesn't do anything like that for me. It just... well, it just settles my mind about whether I've lost those papers. Once I see them, I know they aren't gone, and that's really the only feeling I get from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be symmetrical or evenly arranged in any way?\n\nPatient: No, they don't need to be symmetrical. Like I mentioned, I really don't care how they look in the folder or the box. They can be crooked or in a messy pile for all I care. I just need to see that they are still there and I haven't misplaced them. The way they're arranged doesn't bother me one bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or unease in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it does help ease my mind for a moment. Even when I... well, even when I know better, this thought will just keep needling me. So, I have to go look just to prove to myself that I didn't make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things because you're trying to control something or prevent a mistake from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just mentioned, it's really just about the mistakes. So I wouldn't say it's about needing control, just making sure I didn't mess up what I already have.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I came in because I seem to be spending far too much of my day checking things. I keep opening the same folders, the drawers, my labeled boxes, just... again and again. \n\nI just have to make sure that my important papers and keepsakes are exactly where I left them. And the frustrating part is, I'll do this even when I just checked them a few minutes before. It's taking up too much of my time, and I needed to speak to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to follow a specific routine or pattern when you're checking these things?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, the pattern is really just... doing the exact same thing over and over. I go to those same folders, the drawers, the boxes I have labeled. I just have to physically see those important papers and my keepsakes to be absolutely sure they're still sitting right where I put them. So, I suppose the routine is just that—going right back to open them and check again, even if I literally just looked at them a few minutes ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I logically know better, a thought will just keep needling me out of nowhere. I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or... or that I put a special family recipe in the wrong box. I just can't seem to let the thought pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless, and that's usually what makes me go back and check.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check these things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm not worried that... that a disaster will strike or someone will get hurt if I don't check. It's not like that. It's really just what I mentioned before. I just get this overwhelming feeling that I've lost something important or made a careless mistake. I don't have any fears about something terrible happening, beyond just the frustration of losing the papers themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to keep your belongings in a very specific order or arrangement?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I have my labeled boxes and folders so I know where things are, but I don't feel like they have to be perfectly lined up or arranged in a certain way. It's just about knowing the items are still inside them, not how they look on the shelf.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like checking these things will prevent something bad from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Well, no, like I was just saying... it's really not about that at all. I don't think checking the boxes is going to save anyone's life or keep someone from getting hurt. I don't have any fears like that. It's just about making sure I haven't lost my husband's papers or my recipes. That's really all it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be in a specific order or position within their containers?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, as long as they're inside the folder or the box, I really don't care how they're stacked. I don't need them alphabetized or facing a certain way. It's just... well, like I said, it's just about seeing that the papers are actually in there and haven't gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way or a certain number of times to feel complete?\n\nPatient: No, I don't count or anything like that. There's no special routine to it. I just open the box or the folder, look inside, and once I see that the papers are still there, I'm done. I don't have to do it a certain number of times to feel right... I just have to see them with my own eyes so I know they aren't lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking helps you feel more balanced or even in some way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I'm not sure what you mean by feeling balanced or even. It doesn't do anything like that for me. It just... well, it just settles my mind about whether I've lost those papers. Once I see them, I know they aren't gone, and that's really the only feeling I get from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be symmetrical or evenly arranged in any way?\n\nPatient: No, they don't need to be symmetrical. Like I mentioned, I really don't care how they look in the folder or the box. They can be crooked or in a messy pile for all I care. I just need to see that they are still there and I haven't misplaced them. The way they're arranged doesn't bother me one bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or unease in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it does help ease my mind for a moment. But I suppose that's exactly why I came in to see you. I spend far too much of my day opening those same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again. I just have to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are where I left them. The problem is, the unease comes right back... and I end up checking them all over again, even when I just checked a few minutes before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things because you're afraid of making a mistake, like misfiling something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that is exactly it. Even when I... well, even when I know better, this thought will just keep needling me. I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or maybe I put a family recipe in the wrong box somewhere. It just gets stuck in my head, and I can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. So, I have to go look just to prove to myself that I didn't make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things because you're trying to control something or prevent a mistake from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just mentioned, it's really just about the mistakes. I don't think I'm trying to... control things in general, exactly. I just get so worried that I've lost something or thrown a paper away by accident. So I wouldn't say it's about needing control, just making sure I didn't mess up what I already have.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. It's not really about everything being... perfect. I don't care if the folders look neat or if the papers are perfectly straight inside the boxes. It's just... well, like I said a moment ago, it's just about knowing I didn't lose them. I just need to see with my own eyes that they are still there and I didn't accidentally throw them out. So, no, I wouldn't say it's about making things perfect at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things because you need to be certain that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose certainty is a good word for it. It's just... I need to be absolutely sure that the important things are still there, that I haven't made a careless error. Until I check and see it for myself, I just can't feel settled. So, yes, I just need to be certain that everything is okay and where it belongs.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just can't seem to let the thought pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless, and that's usually what makes me go back and check.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check these things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm not worried that... that a disaster will strike or someone will get hurt if I don't check. It's not like that. It's really just what I mentioned before. I just get this overwhelming feeling that I've lost something important or made a careless mistake. I don't have any fears about something terrible happening, beyond just the frustration of losing the papers themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to keep your belongings in a very specific order or arrangement?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I have my labeled boxes and folders so I know where things are, but I don't feel like they have to be perfectly lined up or arranged in a certain way. It's just about knowing the items are still inside them, not how they look on the shelf.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like checking these things will prevent something bad from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Well, no, like I was just saying... it's really not about that at all. I don't think checking the boxes is going to save anyone's life or keep someone from getting hurt. I don't have any fears like that. It's just about making sure I haven't lost my husband's papers or my recipes. That's really all it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be in a specific order or position within their containers?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, as long as they're inside the folder or the box, I really don't care how they're stacked. I don't need them alphabetized or facing a certain way. It's just... well, like I said, it's just about seeing that the papers are actually in there and haven't gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way or a certain number of times to feel complete?\n\nPatient: No, I don't count or anything like that. There's no special routine to it. I just open the box or the folder, look inside, and once I see that the papers are still there, I'm done. I don't have to do it a certain number of times to feel right... I just have to see them with my own eyes so I know they aren't lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking helps you feel more balanced or even in some way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I'm not sure what you mean by feeling balanced or even. It doesn't do anything like that for me. It just... well, it just settles my mind about whether I've lost those papers. Once I see them, I know they aren't gone, and that's really the only feeling I get from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the items you're checking need to be symmetrical or evenly arranged in any way?\n\nPatient: No, they don't need to be symmetrical. Like I mentioned, I really don't care how they look in the folder or the box. They can be crooked or in a messy pile for all I care. I just need to see that they are still there and I haven't misplaced them. The way they're arranged doesn't bother me one bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or unease in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it does help ease my mind for a moment. But I suppose that's exactly why I came in to see you. I spend far too much of my day opening those same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again. I just have to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are where I left them. The problem is, the unease comes right back... and I end up checking them all over again, even when I just checked a few minutes before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things because you're afraid of making a mistake, like misfiling something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that is exactly it. Even when I... well, even when I know better, this thought will just keep needling me. I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or maybe I put a family recipe in the wrong box somewhere. It just gets stuck in my head, and I can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. So, I have to go look just to prove to myself that I didn't make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things because you're trying to control something or prevent a mistake from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just mentioned, it's really just about the mistakes. I don't think I'm trying to... control things in general, exactly. I just get so worried that I've lost something or thrown a paper away by accident. So I wouldn't say it's about needing control, just making sure I didn't mess up what I already have.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. It's not really about everything being... perfect. I don't care if the folders look neat or if the papers are perfectly straight inside the boxes. It's just... well, like I said a moment ago, it's just about knowing I didn't lose them. I just need to see with my own eyes that they are still there and I didn't accidentally throw them out. So, no, I wouldn't say it's about making things perfect at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things because you need to be certain that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose certainty is a good word for it. It's just... I need to be absolutely sure that the important things are still there, that I haven't made a careless error. Until I check and see it for myself, I just can't feel settled. So, yes, I just need to be certain that everything is okay and where it belongs.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking these things has anything to do with making sure things are just right or exact in some way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not about them being just right or lined up a certain way. I don't care how they look in the box or if they're perfectly exact. Like I was saying, it's really just about knowing the paper is actually in there and not in the trash. I just need to see that it's safe. So, no, it's not about making things exact.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The problem is, the unease comes right back... and I end up checking them all over again, even when I just checked a few minutes before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things because you're afraid of making a mistake, like misfiling something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that is exactly it. Even when I... well, even when I know better, this thought will just keep needling me. I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or maybe I put a family recipe in the wrong box somewhere. It just gets stuck in my head, and I can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. So, I have to go look just to prove to myself that I didn't make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things because you're trying to control something or prevent a mistake from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just mentioned, it's really just about the mistakes. I don't think I'm trying to... control things in general, exactly. I just get so worried that I've lost something or thrown a paper away by accident. So I wouldn't say it's about needing control, just making sure I didn't mess up what I already have.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. It's not really about everything being... perfect. I don't care if the folders look neat or if the papers are perfectly straight inside the boxes. It's just... well, like I said a moment ago, it's just about knowing I didn't lose them. I just need to see with my own eyes that they are still there and I didn't accidentally throw them out. So, no, I wouldn't say it's about making things perfect at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things because you need to be certain that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose certainty is a good word for it. It's just about making sure I haven't lost my husband's papers or my recipes. It's just... well, like I said, it's just about seeing that the papers are actually in there and haven't gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way or a certain number of times to feel complete?\n\nPatient: No, I don't count or anything like that. There's no special routine to it. I just open the box or the folder, look inside, and once I see that the papers are still there, I'm done. I don't have to do it a certain number of times to feel right... I just have to see them with my own eyes so I know they aren't lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking helps you feel more balanced or even in some way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I'm not sure what you mean by feeling balanced or even. It doesn't do anything like that for me. It just... well, it just settles my mind about whether I've lost those papers. Even when I... well, even when I know better, this thought will just keep needling me. I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or maybe I put a family recipe in the wrong box somewhere. It just gets stuck in my head, and I can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. So, I have to go look just to prove to myself that I didn't make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things because you're trying to control something or prevent a mistake from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just mentioned, it's really just about the mistakes. I don't think I'm trying to... control things in general, exactly. I just get so worried that I've lost something or thrown a paper away by accident. So I wouldn't say it's about needing control, just making sure I didn't mess up what I already have.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. So, no, I wouldn't say it's about making things perfect at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things because you need to be certain that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose certainty is a good word for it. It's just... I need to be absolutely sure that the important things are still there, that I haven't made a careless error. Until I check and see it for myself, I just can't feel settled. So, no, it's not about making things exact.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking if someone you trust assured you that everything is in its place?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I suppose I don't know if someone else telling me it's fine would actually stop the worry. I feel like I might still need to see it with my own two eyes just to be safe, but I really couldn't say for certain since it hasn't come up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check these things if you were absolutely certain you hadn't made a mistake?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that.
